\subsection{Misra-Gries (MG)}\label{sec:mg_background}
As shown in \Cref{fig:mg_overview}, MG consists of an array of~$w$ slots.
Each slot stores a full key (shown in gray) along with a dedicated counter (shown in white).
To insert a key~$x$, MG first checks whether~$x$ is already stored in the array.
If so, it increments~$x$'s counter.
Otherwise, if the array contains an empty slot, MG stores~$x$ in that slot with a count of~1.
If~$x$ is not present and the array is full, MG decrements all counters, and it evicts any key whose counter reaches zero, freeing its slot.
If at least one slot is freed, MG admits the incoming key~$x$ into the array with a count of~1.
Otherwise, the key~$x$ is discarded.
A query for a key currently stored in the array returns that key's counter as the frequency estimate, while a query for an untracked key returns zero.
\Cref{fig:mg_overview} illustrates these \mbox{insertion and query cases}.

\textbf{Properties.}
MG's design gives rise to three useful properties relative to CMS and CS.
First, because a key's counter is incremented only when that key itself is inserted, MG never overestimates its frequency, though it may underestimate it due to the global decrement operations.
Second, MG tends to retain the stream's heavy hitters because these keys accumulate enough increments to survive repeated global decrements.
Under skew, these heavy hitters accrue many more increments than global decrements, making their underestimation error small relative to their true frequencies.
Third, because MG stores full keys, it can directly report the keys corresponding to the heavy hitters.
This is unlike CMS and CS, which hash keys into counters without retaining the keys themselves, and therefore cannot directly identify which keys are heavy hitters.
This reporting capability is useful, for example, for identifying network flows that consume the most bandwidth~\cite{HeavyGuardian,HeavyKeeper,WavingSketch} or unusually frequent events that may indicate anomalies~\cite{NetworkAnomalyDetection1,NetworkAnomalyDetection2}.

\textbf{Deletions.}
An intrinsic limitation of MG relative to CMS and CS is that it cannot support deletions without weakening its error bound~\cite{BatchUpdateMisraGries,SpaceSavingBoundedDeletes}.
This is because MG retains dedicated counters for only a bounded set of candidate heavy hitters.
If the tracked heavy hitters are deleted, previously untracked keys may become heavy hitters, but MG has discarded their historical frequencies.

\textbf{Estimation Error.}
Increasing the number of slots~$w$ allows MG to retain more keys, thereby making global decrements less frequent and improving estimation accuracy.
With~$w$ slots, MG guarantees an additive underestimation error of at most~$N/w$, i.e., $0 \leq f(x)-\hat{f}(x) \leq N/w$ for any key~$x$.
This bound holds because each global decrement subtracts one from~$w$ stored counters and can therefore be ``charged'' against~$w$ stream insertions.
Since there are~$N$ insertions in total, at most~$N/w$ global decrements can occur, so the estimate of any key can fall below its true frequency by at most~$N/w$.
As this counting argument is deterministic, MG's error bound always holds with confidence~1, unlike the probabilistic error bounds of CMS and CS.
\Cref{tab:conventional_sketch_stats} compares MG with CMS and CS.
As shown, MG's worst-case estimation error grows linearly with the stream length, similarly to CMS and CS.

\textbf{Classic Implementation.}
MG is typically implemented using a hash table that maps each tracked key to its counter, supporting constant-time queries and insertions of already-tracked keys.
Inserting an untracked key may trigger a global decrement, requiring a scan of all~$\Theta(w)$ counters.
However, as observed in the original MG paper~\cite{MisraGries}, the error bound of~$N/w$ implies that at most~$N/w$ global decrements can occur over a stream of~$N$ insertions.
Consequently, global decrements occur at most once every~$w$ insertions on average, amortizing the~$\Theta(w)$ scan cost of each to~$O(1)$ per insertion.
Thus, when implemented using a hash table, MG supports insertions in constant expected amortized time.
The first row of \Cref{tab:mg_performance_space_overhead} summarizes the properties of this \mbox{scan-based approach}.

\textbf{De-amortizing Decrements.}
In the above implementation, an individual insertion can take~$\Theta(w)$ time to scan and decrement all counters.
This can be problematic for applications with strict latency requirements, such as network routers that must aggregate traffic statistics without delaying packet forwarding~\cite{UnivMon,StingySketch}.
To avoid this worst-case cost, many MG implementations perform global decrements lazily using a global frame of reference~$L$, which is incremented whenever a decrement is triggered.
Each stored counter value~$v$ then represents an effective count of~$v-L$, while newly admitted keys are assigned~$L+1$, corresponding to an effective count of~1.

\textbf{De-amortizing Evictions.}
The remaining challenge is efficiently identifying entries whose effective counts reach zero so they can be evicted.
Existing MG implementations tackle this challenge using auxiliary data structures.
Heap-based implementations~\cite{MGH1,MGH2,BatchUpdateMisraGries} maintain a min-heap that orders keys by their counts.
The minimum element identifies the next eviction victim, but maintaining the heap increases the cost of every insertion to~$O(\log w)$, sacrificing the constant amortized insertion time of the scan-based approach.
A more efficient approach is to maintain a doubly linked list that organizes keys by their counter values~\cite{SpaceSaving,UnbiasedSpaceSaving}.
Each node in this linked list corresponds to a distinct counter value and points to all keys in the hash table with that count.
Incrementing a counter moves its key by at most one position in the linked list, enabling constant-time updates, while keys with zero-valued counters appear at the head and can be evicted in constant time.

However, both min-heap and doubly linked list approaches incur significant space overhead.
Each entry in the auxiliary structure must store a pointer to its corresponding hash-table entry to identify eviction victims, while each hash-table entry must store a pointer back to the auxiliary structure to support updates.
The doubly linked list additionally requires two pointers per node to link neighboring counter values.
Each pointer requires at least~$\log_2 w$ bits to address one of at least~$w$ entries in the hash table, heap, or linked list.
As a result, these auxiliary structures increase the sketch's memory footprint by~2--3$\times$, consuming space that could otherwise be used to store a larger hash table and thereby improve accuracy.
The second and third rows of \Cref{tab:mg_performance_space_overhead} summarize the properties of these two approaches when also coupled with lazy decrements.

\subsection{\sketchmg}\label{sec:mg_sublime}
Similarly to CMS and CS, a shortcoming of classic MG designs is their use of fixed-length counters: under skewed workloads, many counters store relatively small values, leaving their higher-order bits unused.
Moreover, the estimation errors of these MG designs grow linearly with the stream length, causing estimates to become increasingly inaccurate as the stream grows.
We now introduce \sketchmg, a generalization of MG that addresses both shortcomings.
It employs \counterencodingabbrv\ to compress the counters, and it should expand the hash table as the stream grows to control the estimation error.
Both techniques are compatible with all MG variants, including those listed in \Cref{tab:mg_performance_space_overhead} \mbox{and others~\cite{UnbiasedSpaceSaving,SpaceSavingBoundedDeletes,HeavyGuardian,HeavyKeeper,WavingSketch}}.

For applications requiring strong worst-case latency guarantees, we observe that the existing de-amortization techniques described above pose two challenges when combined with \counterencodingabbrv.
First, recall that the global frame of reference enables performing decrements in constant time without immediately updating every individual counter.
However, as decrements accumulate, stored counter values can become substantially larger than their effective counts, inflating the space consumed by their \counterencodingabbrv\ encodings.
We address this issue by gradually applying accumulated decrements to individual counters over subsequent insertions.
This keeps the stored counter values close to their effective counts, preserving compact \counterencodingabbrv\ encodings while avoiding costly bulk updates.

Second, conventional data structures for de-amortizing evictions, such as min-heaps and linked lists, incur substantial memory overhead.
This reduces the space available for storing entries in the hash table, thereby limiting estimation accuracy.
In the context of \sketchmg, this overhead would also overshadow the space savings achieved by \counterencodingabbrv.
To address this challenge, we introduce a compressed segment tree that identifies eviction candidates using only two additional bits per counter, reducing the overall memory footprint by~2--3$\times$ compared with conventional heap- or linked-list-based designs.
The segment tree guarantees worst-case insertion time logarithmic in the sketch size, though we show that it also preserves~$O(1)$ amortized insertion time because counter updates rarely propagate far up the tree.

\textbf{Accommodating Skew with \counterencodingabbrv.}
Recall from \Cref{sec:accommodating_skew} that \counterencodingabbrv\ compresses contiguous counters by packing them into the same cache line along with their extensions.
\counterencodingabbrv\ therefore applies directly to CMS and CS, which consist purely of contiguous arrays of counters.
MG, however, stores keys and counters together within a hash table, making \counterencodingabbrv\ less straightforward to apply.

To address this, \sketchmg separates the counters from the keys.
It stores the keys in a contiguous array of slots, managing them using an open-addressed hash table scheme.
Our implementation uses a Cuckoo hash table~\cite{CuckooHashing,CuckooHashingConference} with four slots per bucket and a maximum load factor of 95\%, though other open-addressing schemes (e.g., linear or quadratic probing) are also applicable.
The contiguous layout of an open-addressed hash table allows \sketchmg to store the counters in a separate array that mirrors the hash table, with one counter per slot.
Specifically, the counter corresponding to a key in the~$i${\nobreakdash-}th hash-table slot is stored at offset~$i$ in the counter array.
Separating the counters in this way allows them to be encoded directly using \counterencodingabbrv, while the mirrored layout enables efficient access to a key's counter.
To save space as the counter values evolve, \sketchmg adaptively retunues \counterencodingabbrv\ using the same procedure as \sketchcms and \sketchcs (described under the paragraph titled ``Adaptive Tuning'' in \Cref{sec:accommodating_skew}).

When hash collisions trigger cuckooing, an existing key may be relocated to a different slot in the hash table.
To preserve mirroring, each key relocation must be accompanied by moving its corresponding counter.
This involves copying the counter's stub and, if present, relocating its extension.
Relocating an extension entails a small number of bitwise operations on the source and destination extension pools, since each extension pool is typically~1--2 machine words long (as discussed in \Cref{sec:accommodating_skew}).
Counter relocation therefore adds only a small constant overhead to insertions in expectation, since the counter movements correspond directly to key movements performed during collision resolution, whose total expected cost per insertion is constant.

A consequence of separating keys and their counters is that accessing a key's counter may incur an additional cache miss.
We mitigate this overhead by speculatively prefetching the regions of the counter array likely to contain a key's counter while searching for it in the hash table.
For a Cuckoo hash table, a key can reside in one of two buckets, so we prefetch the counters corresponding to both buckets in addition to prefetching the buckets themselves.

\iftods
In \Cref{thm:all_counters_space} of \Cref{sec:memory_analysis}, we prove that \counterencodingabbrv\ encodes \sketchmg's counters using at most~$4.41 + 1.26 \cdot \log_2 (N/w)$ bits per counter while achieving significantly lower space consumption under skew.
\else
In \Cref{thm:all_counters_space}, we prove that \counterencodingabbrv\ encodes \sketchmg's counters using at most~$4.41 + 1.26 \cdot \log_2 (N/w)$ bits per counter while achieving significantly lower space consumption under skew.
\fi
In contrast, conventional fixed-length counters each use at least~$\log_2 N$ bits regardless of the workload.
The rightmost column of \Cref{tab:mg_performance_space_overhead} compares the space consumption of \sketchmg's counters with that of conventional MG implementations.

\textbf{Accommodating Unbounded Data Growth with Expansions.}
As discussed earlier, inserting a key that is already tracked by MG simply increments its counter and does not increase the estimation error.
MG's error grows through global decrements triggered by insertions of untracked keys, with each decrement increasing the worst-case error by one.
Moreover, inserting an untracked key into an empty slot without triggering a global decrement may contribute to future estimation error, as the newly admitted key may be infrequent and occupy a slot that could otherwise be used to track a heavy hitter.
These observations motivate a different expansion criterion from those used by \sketchcms and \sketchcs.
Whereas \sketchcms expands based on the total number of insertions and \sketchcs on the stream's~$\ell_2$-norm, \sketchmg should expand based on the number of untracked-key insertions observed so far, denoted by~$M$.
This is because the number of untracked-key insertions governs the estimation error.

Thus, we specify the number of counters \sketchmg should expand to accommodate after~$M$ untracked-key insertions by defining a size function~$W(M)$.
As in Sections~\ref{sec:accommodating_unknown_stream_lengths} and \ref{sec:cs}, we expose a wide range of accuracy--memory trade-offs by defining the size function as a sublinear power of the form~$W(M)=M^\alpha/\epsilon$.
The parameter~$\epsilon$ determines \sketchmg's initial size of~$1/\epsilon$ counters.
When~$\epsilon>1$, \sketchmg still starts with one counter but delays its first expansion, trading estimation accuracy for a smaller memory footprint.
The parameter~$\alpha$ controls how quickly subsequent expansions occur as the number of untracked-key insertions~$M$ increases.
For~$0<\alpha<1$, \sketchmg doubles in size whenever~$M$ more than doubles, whereas for~$\alpha=1$, it doubles in size whenever~$M$ doubles.
Setting~$\alpha=0$ prevents \sketchmg from expanding, recovering the classic \mbox{fixed-size MG}.

\iftods
Using sublinear power size functions (with~$0 \leq \alpha < 1$), \sketchmg achieves the same asymptotic accuracy--memory trade-off as \sketchcms and \sketchcs.
\else
When using sublinear power size functions (with~$0 \leq \alpha < 1$), \sketchmg achieves the same asymptotic accuracy--memory trade-off as \sketchcms and \sketchcs.
\fi
The following theorem, proven in Appendix~\ref{apx:upper_bounds_mg_normal}, formalizes this result:
\begin{restatable}{theorem}{thmmgnormal}
    \label{thm:mg-normal} 
    When using a sublinear power size function of the form~$W(M)=M^\alpha/\epsilon$ on a stream where a total of~$M$ insertions of untracked keys occur, \sketchmg provides an estimation error~$E(M)$ satisfying
    $$
    E(M) \leq  
    \begin{cases}
        \epsilon M & \text{$\alpha=0$,} \\
        O(1) \cdot \epsilon {M}^{1-\alpha} & \text{$0<\alpha<1$,} \\
        \ln M \cdot \epsilon & \text{$\alpha=1$ \textup{and} $\epsilon>1$,} \\
        0 & \text{$\alpha=1$ \textup{and} $\epsilon \leq 1$.}
    \end{cases}
    $$
\end{restatable}
\noindent
This theorem shows that when~$\alpha=0$, \sketchmg's error bounds match those of a classic fixed-size MG.
When~$0<\alpha<1$, despite not knowing how large the number of untracked-key insertions~$M$ would grow, \sketchmg achieves error bounds within a small constant factor of those of a clairvoyantly sized MG, i.e., an MG allocated upfront with~$W(M)$ counters given prior knowledge of~$M$.
We quantify this constant factor shortly.

When~$\alpha=1$ and~$\epsilon\leq1$, \sketchmg trivially achieves zero additive error because it expands with every untracked-key insertion, effectively becoming a conventional hash table that stores every key and tracks its exact count.
This is captured by the bottom case of \Cref{thm:mg-normal}, which we include for completeness.
When~$\alpha=1$ and $\epsilon>1$, \sketchmg instead expands proportionally to the number of untracked-key insertions, but maintains too few entries to track every key.
One might hope that this would still yield constant additive error, as suggested by extrapolating the second case of \Cref{thm:mg-normal} to~$\alpha=1$.
However, as with \sketchcms and \sketchcs, the additive error grows logarithmically rather than remaining constant, as shown by the third case of the theorem.
The memory footprint lower bound in \Cref{thm:sketch_expandability_special_case} establishes that this trade-off is fundamental to dynamically expanding sketches with MG's deterministic error guarantees.

Similarly to \sketchcms, \sketchmg defaults to~$\alpha=0.5$ in its size function to balance estimation error and memory footprint, making both scale as~$O(\sqrt{M})$.
To tune the parameters~$\alpha$ and~$\epsilon$ for a particular workload, practitioners can substitute the stream length~$N$ for~$M$ in \Cref{thm:mg-normal} to obtain an upper bound on the estimation error, since~$N \geq M$.
At runtime, \sketchmg tracks the actual number of untracked-key insertions~$M$ and can use it to report a \mbox{tighter error bound}.

\textbf{Fine-Grained Expansions.}
Unlike \sketchcms and \sketchcs, which expand by doubling the size of their arrays, \sketchmg can expand by factors smaller than~2.
This is because it uses a hash table rather than flat counter arrays, allowing it to increase its capacity by rehashing the stored keys into a larger table.
Our key insight is that such finer-grained expansions yield tighter error bounds for \sketchmg.
By adding counters more gradually, \sketchmg can expand sooner, reducing the frequency of evictions and thereby preserving more information about the historical frequencies of tracked keys.

Thus, we augment \sketchmg with a fine-grained expansion mechanism.
Starting with four slots per bucket in our Cuckoo hash table, each expansion rebuilds the table with one additional slot per bucket while keeping the number of buckets unchanged.
Once the bucket size reaches eight slots, we instead double the number of buckets and restore the original bucket size of four slots, redistributing the entries of each original bucket across two new buckets, similarly to extendible hashing~\cite{ExtendibleHashing}.
Each expansion is triggered whenever the size function~$W(M)$ exceeds the hash table's current maximum capacity.
Alternative techniques for fine-grained hash table expansion, such as linear hashing~\cite{LinearHashing,VirtualHashing} or stretching~\cite{Zeno}, are also applicable.

Compared with doubling the sketch's size, this fine-grained expansion scheme rebuilds the sketch four times as often.
Nevertheless, it allows \sketchmg to add counters sooner than \sketchcms and \sketchcs, yielding smaller constant factors in its error bounds.
\Cref{thm:mg-normal}, proven in Appendix~\ref{apx:upper_bounds_mg_normal}, formalizes these improvements.
In particular, when~$\alpha=1$, \sketchmg's error bound involves a natural logarithm, which is smaller than the base-2 logarithm appearing in the corresponding bounds for \sketchcms (\Cref{thm:cms-w}) and \sketchcs (\Cref{thm:cs-w}).
\iftods
Moreover, when~$\alpha<1$, the constant factor hidden by the asymptotics of \Cref{thm:mg-normal}\footnote{
    Appendix~\ref{apx:upper_bounds_mg_normal} shows that this factor is close to~$\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1} \cdot \left[ \left( N^\alpha/\epsilon \right)^{2 \cdot (\frac1\alpha+1)} \cdot {}_2F_1\left( 1, \frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}; \frac{1+2\alpha}{\alpha}; -N^\alpha/\epsilon \right) \vphantom{\frac{(N^\alpha/\epsilon \cdot (N^\alpha/\epsilon-1))^{\frac1\alpha+1}}2} \right. -{}$ $\left. \frac{(N^\alpha/\epsilon \cdot (N^\alpha/\epsilon-1))^{\frac1\alpha+1}}2 \cdot {}_2F_1\left( 1, \frac{1+\alpha}\alpha; \frac{1+2\alpha}\alpha; -\frac{N^\alpha/\epsilon-1}{2} \right) \right]$, where~${}_2F_1$ is Gauss's hypergeometric function.
    This quantity can be shown to be at most~$\frac{1}{1-\alpha}$, and it converges to~$\ln M$ as~$\alpha$ approaches~1.
} is at most~$1/(1-\alpha)$, compared with~$\frac{2^{1/\alpha}-1}{2^{1/\alpha-1}-1}$.
\else
Moreover, when~$\alpha<1$, the constant factor hidden by the asymptotics of \Cref{thm:mg-normal}\footnote{
    In the proof of \Cref{thm:mg-normal} in Appendix~\ref{apx:upper_bounds_mg_normal}, we show that this constant factor is close to the expression~$\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1} \cdot \left[ \left( N^\alpha/\epsilon \right)^{2 \cdot (\frac1\alpha+1)} \cdot {}_2F_1\left( 1, \frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}; \frac{1+2\alpha}{\alpha}; -N^\alpha/\epsilon \right) \vphantom{\frac{(N^\alpha/\epsilon \cdot (N^\alpha/\epsilon-1))^{\frac1\alpha+1}}2} \right. -{}$ $\left. \frac{(N^\alpha/\epsilon \cdot (N^\alpha/\epsilon-1))^{\frac1\alpha+1}}2 \cdot {}_2F_1\left( 1, \frac{1+\alpha}\alpha; \frac{1+2\alpha}\alpha; -\frac{N^\alpha/\epsilon-1}{2} \right) \right]$, where~${}_2F_1$ denotes Gauss's hypergeometric function.
    This quantity can be shown to be at most~$\frac{1}{1-\alpha}$, and that it converges to $\ln M$ as $\alpha$ approaches~1.
} is at most~$1/(1-\alpha)$, compared with~$\frac{2^{1/\alpha}-1}{2^{1/\alpha-1}-1}$.
\fi
Under the default setting of~$\alpha=0.5$, these factors evaluate to~2 and~3.

\textbf{Tail Guarantees.}
As with CMS and CS, MG also provides a tail guarantee: any key's estimation error is at most~$\min_{1 \leq i \leq w} \frac{M^{\mathrm{res}(i)}}{w-i+1}$, where~$M^{\mathrm{res}(i)}$ is the total number of insertions for untracked keys that are not among the~$i$ most frequent.
The following theorem, proven in Appendix~\ref{apx:upper_bounds_mg_res}, shows that \sketchmg improves upon this tail bound with the same sublinear scaling as in \Cref{thm:mg-normal}:
\begin{restatable}{theorem}{thmmgres}
    \label{thm:mg-res}
    When using a sublinear power size function of the form~$W(M)=M^\alpha/\epsilon$ on a stream where a total of~$M$ insertions of untracked keys occur, \sketchmg provides an estimation error~$E(M)$ satisfying
    $$
    E(M) \leq  
    \begin{cases}
        \epsilon M^{\textup{res}(k)} & \text{$\alpha=0$,} \\
        O(1) \cdot \epsilon \parentheses{M^{\textup{res}(k)}}^{1-\alpha} + O(k^{1/\alpha-1}) & \text{$0<\alpha<1$,} \\
        \ln M^{\textup{res}(k)} \cdot \epsilon + \ln k  & \text{$\alpha=1$ \textup{and} $\epsilon > 1$,} \\
        0  & \text{$\alpha=1$ \textup{and} $\epsilon \leq 1$,}
    \end{cases}
    $$
    where~$M^{\textup{res}(k)}$ is the total number of untracked-key insertions of all keys not among the top~$k$.
\end{restatable}
The additive terms on the right side of the above error bounds arise because \sketchmg may initially have fewer than~$k$ counters.
This forces \sketchmg to ``wait out'' the stream until the hash table's capacity reaches~$k$, introducing the additive error.
These terms vanish if \sketchmg is allocated~$k$ counters from the start.

\textbf{Incremental Counter Deflation.}
Similarly to classic MG designs, \sketchmg de-amortizes global decrement operations using a global frame of reference~$L$, allowing each to be performed in constant time.
A consequence of this approach is that the stored value of each counter can exceed its effective count.
This causes counters to occupy more bits than necessary when encoded with \counterencodingabbrv, reducing its space savings.
A naive way of addressing this problem would be to periodically scan the entire structure and subtract~$L$ from every counter.
However, each such scan would take time linear in the sketch size~$w$, defeating the purpose of de-amortization.

Instead, we incrementally deflate the stored counter values to bring them closer to their effective counts.
We do this by partitioning the counters into blocks of~$\log_2 w$ counters each (this block size bounds both the worst-case running time and the metadata overhead, as explained below).
Each block maintains a \emph{local frame of reference} that records the last value of the global frame of reference applied to that block.
A counter's effective count is then obtained by subtracting its block's outstanding decrements (i.e., the difference between the global and local frames of reference) from its stored value.
When a counter is relocated to mirror a key's movement in the Cuckoo hash table, \sketchmg preserves its effective count by adjusting its stored value to account for the difference in outstanding decrements between the source and destination blocks.

With each global decrement, \sketchmg applies the outstanding decrements to one block, removing each counter's corresponding key from the hash table if its count reaches zero.
It then advances to the next block in circular order, which it processes upon the next global decrement.
Because there are~$w/\log_2 w$ blocks, each block is visited once every~$w/\log_2 w$ global decrements and thus accumulates at most~$w/\log_2 w$ outstanding decrements.
This allows both the global frame of reference~$L$ and the local frames of reference to wrap around modulo~$w/\log_2 w$, enabling each to be stored in at most~$\log_2 w$ bits.
Since each local frame of reference is shared by the~$\log_2 w$ counters in its block, the total metadata overhead is only~1 bit per counter.
At the same time, applying the outstanding decrements to a block takes only~$O(\log w)$ worst-case time.
This also preserves~$O(1)$ amortized insertion time, since a block is processed upon a global decrement, which occurs at most once every~$w$ insertions on average, as established in \Cref{sec:mg_background}.

\textbf{Evictions via a Segment Tree.}
To de-amortize the cost of identifying eviction candidates, we design a compact segment tree~\cite{SegmentTree1,SegmentTree2,CG}.
This segment tree is a full binary tree whose leaves are the counters in \sketchmg's counter array, as shown in \Cref{fig:mg_segment_tree}.
Each internal node identifies the counter with the smallest non-zero effective count in its subtree by storing its offset relative to the subtree's leftmost leaf.
We exclude zero effective counts because they correspond to overprovisioned empty slots in the hash table that help speed up insertions.
The segment tree's root therefore stores the offset of the smallest non-zero count in the entire counter array, allowing \sketchmg to immediately identify an eviction victim.

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \begin{tikzpicture}

        \def\arraylen{8}
        \def\arraylenminusone{7}
        \def\cellw{0.75}
        \def\arrayw{\arraylen*\cellw}
        \def\arraygap{0.1}
        \def\annotationoffset{0.22}
        \def\bottomymul{3.1}
        \def\casebracewidth{1.5}
        \def\casebracewidthquery{0.55}
        \def\treediffy{0.25}
        \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\treelevels}{round(log2(\arraylen))}

        \def\counterarray{{7,9,8,4,0,1,2,3}}
        \pgfmathsetmacro{\keyy}{-\arrayh-\arraygap}
        \foreach \i in {1, ..., \arraylen} {
            % Key array (bottom)
            \ifnum\i=5
                \fill[pattern=north east lines,pattern color=gray!60] (\i*\cellw-\cellw,\keyy+0.5*\arrayh) rectangle (\i*\cellw,\keyy-0.5*\arrayh);
            \else

            \fi

            % Counter array (top)

        \fi

        \else
            \draw[decorate,decoration={brace,raise=3pt,amplitude=2pt}] (5*\cellw,0 |- slot-offset-4.south) -- (4*\cellw,0 |- slot-offset-4.south)
                node[pos=0.5,below=6pt,inner sep=1pt] {\small Empty Slot};
        \fi

        % Segment Tree
        \foreach \l in {1, ..., \treelevels} {
            \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\subtreesize}{2^\l}
            \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\levellen}{\arraylen/\subtreesize}

                \else

                \fi

        \iftods
            \node[inner sep=1pt,left=22pt of tree-3-1,yshift=1.075pt,align=left] (global_offset_label) {\footnotesize \textcolor{gray!80}{Min Value:} \\[-4pt] \footnotesize Subtree-Local Offset of Min:};
            \draw[-stealth] ($(tree-3-1.south east)+(0,5.75pt)$) -- ++(0.6,0) node[right,inner sep=1pt] {\footnotesize Stored Binary Encoding};
        \else
            \node[inner sep=1pt,left=22pt of tree-3-1,yshift=1.075pt,align=left] (global_offset_label) {\small \textcolor{gray!80}{Min Value:} \\[-4pt] \small Subtree-Local Offset of Min:};
            \draw[-stealth] ($(tree-3-1.south east)+(0,5.75pt)$) -- ++(0.6,0) node[right,inner sep=1pt,align=center] {\small Stored Binary \\[-4pt] \small Encoding};
        \fi
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \caption{By storing a compressed segment tree, \sketchmg improves the worst-case insertion cost to logarithmic while retaining a constant amortized cost.
    To simplify the figure, we present the effective count of each key in the corresponding counter.}
    \label{fig:mg_segment_tree}
\end{figure}

\Cref{fig:mg_segment_tree} illustrates these offsets in black and the corresponding counter values in gray.
The lowest internal nodes store offsets~0, 1, 1, and 0, indicating which of their two children contain the smaller non-zero counter.
The third node from the left in this level stores~1 rather than~0 because its left child corresponds to an empty slot, as indicated by its zero effective count.
The two internal nodes in the level above store offsets~3 and 1, identifying counters with values~4 and 1 as the minima of their respective subtrees.
Finally, the root stores offset~5, identifying the counter with the smallest non-zero value of~1 in the entire array.

Since an internal node at height~$h$ has at most~$2^h$ leaves in its subtree, its subtree-local offset lies in the range~$[0,2^h)$ and can therefore be encoded in~$h$ bits.
The binary numbers in \Cref{fig:mg_segment_tree} illustrate this encoding.
Because there are at most~$w/2^h$ internal nodes at height~$h$, the total memory required for the subtree-local offsets is at most~$\sum_h h \cdot w/2^h \leq 2 \cdot w$ bits.
Thus, the segment tree incurs a memory overhead that approaches~2 bits per counter from below.
\Cref{tab:mg_performance_space_overhead} compares the combined space overhead of \sketchmg's segment tree and local frames of references with that of the auxiliary structures in classic MG implementations.

\sketchmg evicts a key by emptying the slot indicated by the segment tree's root and resetting its counter to zero.
It then updates the path from the corresponding leaf toward the root to reflect the eviction, taking~$O(\log w)$ time in the worst case.
The traversal terminates as soon as it reaches an internal node whose subtree minimum remains unchanged.
The same update procedure is used when an insertion increments the counter of a tracked key or when a counter is relocated within the array to mirror a key's movement during hash table collision resolution.
In contrast, a global decrement does not require updating the segment tree, since it affects all counters equally.

These update traversals preserve constant amortized insertion time for three reasons:
(1)~Evictions and their corresponding traversals occur only upon global decrements, which happen at most once every~$w$ insertions on average, as discussed in \Cref{sec:mg_background}.
Thus, the~$O(\log w)$ traversal cost amortizes to less than a constant per insertion.
(2)~For counter increments, updates to the segment tree's internal nodes are infrequent.
To see why, we observe that the minimum counter value represented by an internal node (shown in gray in \Cref{fig:mg_segment_tree}) can increase only after every leaf in its subtree has been incremented.
For a node at height~$h$, each such increase can therefore be charged to at least~$2^h$ leaf increments within its subtree.
Equivalently, each leaf increment incurs an amortized cost of at most~$2^{-h}$ for updating its ancestor at height~$h$.
Summing over all ancestors gives a total amortized cost of at most~$\sum_{h=1}^{\infty}2^{-h}=1$ per leaf increment.
(3)~When a Cuckoo hash table moves a key to resolve a collision, the key being moved is effectively chosen at random from the table.
The corresponding counter is therefore also chosen at random from the counter array, so its update traversal advances only one level up the segment tree in expectation.
A similar argument bounds the expected traversal cost for other collision resolution strategies (e.g., linear probing).
The last row of \Cref{tab:mg_performance_space_overhead} summarizes the properties of using this segment tree for evictions.

\textbf{Weighted Updates.}
Certain network applications benefit from sketches that support weighted updates, where a key's frequency can increase by an arbitrary amount in a single insertion~\cite{BatchUpdateMisraGries}.
MG implementations that use doubly linked lists to handle evictions do not support such updates efficiently.
This is because a large increment to an already-tracked key may require traversing the entire linked list to move the key from its beginning to its end, taking time linear in the sketch size.
In contrast, \sketchmg's segment tree supports weighted updates in logarithmic time, as do classic heap-based MG implementations.
This complexity is optimal among MG implementations that rely on comparison-based data structures such as segment trees~\cite{MisraGries}.

\textbf{Applicability to Other MG Variants.}
MG has several variants with different estimation and update properties.
Randomized variants can provide unbiased estimates by probabilistically evicting the least frequent key when an untracked key arrives~\cite{UnbiasedSpaceSaving,WavingSketch}.
Other variants improve practical accuracy at the cost of permitting overestimation~\cite{SpaceSaving,HeavyGuardian,HeavyKeeper}.
Limited support for deletions is also possible at the cost of a larger memory footprint~\cite{SpaceSavingBoundedDeletes}.
The techniques described in this section for applying \sketch\ to MG can be readily adapted to these variants.


\section{Memory Analysis}\label{sec:memory_analysis}
So far in Section~\ref{sec:sublime}, we characterized
\sketch's error with respect to the number of counters it uses. We now
incorporate \counterencodingabbrv\ into the analysis to prove an upper bound on
\sketch's memory footprint in bits
(\Cref{thm:all_counters_space}). We also prove a memory footprint
lower bound for any FE sketch that processes a growing stream without knowledge
of its ultimate length (Theorems~\ref{thm:sketch_expandability_special_case}
and \ref{thm:sketch_expandability}). This lower
bound is parameterized by the sketch's accuracy. We observe that \sketch's
upper bound is within a small logarithmic factor of this lower bound,
demonstrating \sketch's ability to give rise to FE sketches that attain a
desired level of accuracy with a close-to-optimal memory~footprint.

\textbf{Upper Bound.}
As described in
\Cref{sec:accommodating_skew}, \sketch\
adaptively tunes \counterencodingabbrv\ to reclaim memory and ensure that a
small fraction of its bits are unused at any time. This implies that the memory
footprint is dominated by the space occupied by the stubs and the extensions.
We quantify this cost for a single counter based on its count as:
\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma:single_counter_space}
    When working with unsigned counters and stubs of length~$s$ bits,
    \counterencodingabbrv\ encodes a counter value~$v$ in $s$ bits if $v < 2^s$
    and in at most $4 + 1.26 \cdot \log_2 v$ bits otherwise. Signed counters
    use one more bit for encoding the sign in both cases.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    If~$v < 2^s$, it fits in its stub and occupies~$s$ bits. Otherwise, its~$s$
    lower-order bits are stored in its stub and its higher-order bits
    encoding~$\lfloor v/2^s \rfloor$ are stored in an extension. This extension
    has~$\lfloor \log_3 \lfloor v/2^s \rfloor \rfloor + 2$ fragments to
    represent the digits and the delimiter. Thus, it occupies
    $$ 2 \cdot (\lfloor \log_3 \lfloor v/2^s \rfloor \rfloor + 2) \leq 2 \cdot (\log_3 2 \cdot \log_2 \lfloor v/2^s \rfloor + 2) \leq 4 - s + 1.26 \cdot \log_2 v $$
    bits. This, added to the~$s$ bits of space consumed by the stub, yields the
    result.
\end{proof}
Applying \Cref{lemma:single_counter_space} to a counter array while
accounting for \counterencodingabbrv's tuning yields the following lemma.
Intuitively, it shows that the worst-case scenario is when the counter values
are similar, i.e., their distribution is uniform. This is because skew gives
\counterencodingabbrv\ more opportunities to save space.
\begin{lemma}
    \label{thm:counter_array_per_counter_space}
    Consider a counter array of size~$w$ and let~$k$ be the sum of its
    counters' absolute values. When the counters are unsigned,
    \counterencodingabbrv\ encodes the array in~$w \cdot \left[ 4.41 + 1.26
    \cdot \log_2 (k/w) \right]$ bits. When the counters are signed, it uses one
    extra bit per counter to encode their signs.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Applying \Cref{lemma:single_counter_space} to every counter
    within the array and summing results in a total memory footprint of~$m \leq
    \sum_i \max(s, 4+1.26 \cdot \log_2 |v_i|)$ bits. To simplify this
    expression, we bound the stub length~$s$ based on the total absolute
    counter values. Recalling \counterencodingabbrv's tuning mechanics from
    \Cref{sec:accommodating_skew},
    \sketch\ uses stubs that are slightly longer than the length of the average
    counter. In fact, it never sets~$s$ to be more than~2 bits longer than the
    average counter length. Formally, this corresponds to~$s \leq 2 +
    \frac{1}{w} \cdot \sum_i \log_2 |v_i|$, where~$v_i$ denote the signed value
    of the~$i${\nobreakdash-}th counter in the array. Since the logarithm is a
    concave function, we use Jensen's inequality~\cite{Jensen} to bound the sum
    on the right-hand side as~$\log_2(\sum_i |v_i|/w)$. Noting that the
    absolute counter values~$|v_i|$ sum to~$k$ yields $s \leq 2+\log_2(k/w) = 4
    + \log_2(k/(4w))$. Plugging into the memory bound from before yields~$m
    \leq \sum_i 4 + \max(\log_2 (k/(4w)), 1.26 \cdot \log_2 |v_i|)$. We further
    bound each individual max term by 
    \ifnotods
    \begin{align*}
      \max & \left( 1.26 \cdot \log_2 \frac{k}{4w}, 1.26 \cdot \log_2|v_i| \right) \\
      & \leq 1.26 \cdot \max\left( \log_2 \frac{k}{4w}, \log_2|v_i| \right) \\ 
      & \leq 1.26 \cdot \log_2\left( \frac{k}{4w} + |v_i| \right),
    \end{align*}
    \else
    $$ \max\left( 1.26 \cdot \log_2 \frac{k}{4w}, 1.26 \cdot \log_2|v_i| \right) \leq 1.26 \cdot \max\left( \log_2 \frac{k}{4w}, \log_2|v_i| \right) \leq 1.26 \cdot \log_2\left( \frac{k}{4w} + |v_i| \right), $$
    \fi
    thus implying~$m \leq \sum_i 4 + 1.26 \cdot \log(k/(4w) + |v_i|)$.
    Rearranging the sum and once again using the concavity of the logarithm to
    apply Jensen's inequality yields the desired result:
    \ifnotods
    \begin{align*}
      m &\leq w \cdot \left[4 + 1.26 \cdot \frac{1}{w} \cdot \sum_i \log_2(k/(4w)+k/w) \right] \\ 
      & \leq w \cdot \left[ 4.41 + 1.26 \cdot \log_2 (k/w) \right]. 
    \end{align*}
    \else
    $$ m \leq w \cdot \left[4 + 1.26 \cdot \frac{1}{w} \cdot \sum_i \log_2(k/(4w)+k/w) \right] \leq w \cdot \left[ 4.41 + 1.26 \cdot \log_2 (k/w) \right]. $$
    \fi
    \vspace{-8pt}
\end{proof}

Setting~$k$ to the stream length~$N$ in \Cref{thm:counter_array_per_counter_space} leads to a memory footprint bound for \sketchmg's counters.
For \sketchcms and \sketchcs, however, we cannot simply set~$k$~to~$N$ and multiply the space bound by the number of arrays~$d$ to derive the memory footprint.
This is because the sum of the absolute values of the counters increases during expansions by copying them.
By accounting for this, we show the following result:
\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm:all_counters_space}
    When using a sublinear power size as the function~$W(N)$, \sketchcms, has a
    memory footprint of~$\approx d \cdot W(N) \cdot \left[ 4.41 + 1.26 \cdot
    \log_2 N/W(N) \right]$. With a linear size function, \sketchcms
    uses~$\approx d \cdot W(N) \cdot \left[4.41 + 1.26 \cdot \log_2(N \log_2
    N/W(N)) \right]$ bits of memory. The same bounds hold for \sketchcs
    with~$W(F)$ instead of $W(N)$ and with an additive overhead of one bit per
    counter.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    By a similar analysis to that of \Cref{thm:cms-w}, the sum of the absolute counter values within each array in \sketch\ when using a sublinear power as the size function is~$\approx N$, modulo a small constant factor.
    This constant factor become especially negligible in the space bound, since it will appear within a logarithm.
    Similarly, when the size function is linear, we have~$k \approx N \log_2 N$.
    Plugging these values into the bound of~\Cref{thm:counter_array_per_counter_space} and multiplying by~$d$ proves the result.
\end{proof}

\textbf{Lower Bound.}
We prove a memory footprint lower bound for FE sketches by reducing them to
filters. That is, we use an FE sketch to implement filter functionality, which
allows us to inherit known memory lower bounds for filtering. A filter is a
compact data structure that answers whether a query key exists in a given set
of keys. A filter never returns a false negative, but it may return a false
positive with a probability called the \emph{False Positive Rate (FPR)}. The
lower the desired FPR is, i.e., the higher the target accuracy level, the
larger the filter's memory footprint must be. It is known that a filter that
supports a set of a growing size of~$n$ with an FPR of~$\delta$ must use at
least~$n \cdot [ \Omega( \log 1/\delta + \log\log n) - \Theta(1)]$ bits of
memory at some point in time~\cite{PaghExpandability}. Thus, an FE sketch that
can implement such a filter must also use at least this much memory.

Our reduction works as follows.
Consider an FE sketch (e.g., CMS).
Suppose this sketch never underestimates a key's count and guarantees an error of at most~$E(N)$ with a confidence of~$1-\delta$ for streams of length~$N$.
We use it to implement a filter with an FPR of~$\delta$ over a set of size~$n=N/(E(N)+1)$.
Specifically, we insert each key in the set~$E(N)+1$ times into the FE sketch.
This causes the FE sketch to return an estimate of at least~$E(N)+1$ during a query to an existing key within the set.
In contrast, the FE sketch's accuracy guarantees imply that a query to a non-existent key returns an estimate of at most~$E(N)$ with a probability of~$1-\delta$.
Thus, by thresholding the estimate by~$E(N)+1$, we return a true positive for existing keys and a false positive for non-existent keys with an FPR of at most~$\delta$.
The following theorem formalizes these intuitions:
\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm:sketch_expandability_special_case}
    Consider an FE sketch with only overestimation errors that processes growing streams with an error bound of~$E(N)$ and a confidence of~$1-\delta$.
    Such a sketch must use at least~$N/E(N) \cdot [\Omega\left( \log 1/\delta + \log\log (N/E(N)) \right) - \Theta(1)]$ bits of memory at some point in time while processing~a~stream.\footnote{
        In its current form, this expression cannot be applied to FE sketches with full confidence (e.g., MG).
        Such sketches have~$\delta=0$, making the term~$\log(1/\delta)$ undefined.
        Redoing the analysis of~\cite{PaghExpandability} for~$\delta=0$ resolves this issue: the term~$\log(1/\delta)$ is replaced by~$\log u$, where~$u \gg N$ is the size of the key universe.
        This also reduces the constant factor multiplier hidden in the asymptotic bound to~1.
    }
\end{theorem}

\Cref{thm:sketch_expandability_special_case} holds for FE sketches that do not underestimate (e.g., CMS).
The same results also apply to FE sketches that never overestimate and have a deterministic error bound (e.g., MG), since such an FE sketch can be converted into one that never underestimates by adding its error bound to each estimate.
However, applying \Cref{thm:sketch_expandability_special_case} to FE sketches that can both overestimate and underestimate (e.g., CS) is non-trivial, as two-sided errors can introduce false negatives and violate filtering semantics.
Nevertheless, by accounting for the estimation variance, we reason about the false negatives and prove in Appendix~\ref{apx:ommitted_proofs_lower} that the same lower bound holds for these sketches:
\begin{restatable}{theorem}{thmsketchexpandabilitylabel}
    \label{thm:sketch_expandability}
    Consider an FE sketch providing estimates with an expected absolute error
    of~$E(N)$ and a variance of~$\delta \cdot (E(N))^2$ for any key when
    processing a stream of size~$N$. Such an FE sketch must use at
    least~$N/E(N) \cdot [\Omega(\log 1/\delta + \log\log (N/E(N))) -
    \Theta(1)]$ bits of memory at some point in time while processing a stream.
\end{restatable}

One can verify from \Cref{thm:all_counters_space} that, when
fixing the confidence using a constant number of arrays~$d$ while using a
sublinear power (resp. linear) size function~$W(N)$ to achieve an error bound
of~$E(N)$, \sketch\ uses a memory of~$O(N/E(N) \cdot \log (N/E(N)))$ (resp.
$O(N/E(N) \cdot \log N \cdot \log\log N)$) bits. This almost matches the memory
lower bound of \Cref{thm:sketch_expandability}
with a slight difference in the logarithmic
terms. One can extend \sketch\ to match these lower bounds by compressing the
counter arrays via coarsely quantizing their counters and applying run-length
encoding. Doing so can potentially reduce space consumption by as much
as~$5\times$ for long streams in practice. These compression techniques may
compromise update and query performance, though we expect to be able to resolve
this degradation using techniques based on rank and
select~\cite{Poppy,FlorianRankandSelect,CompactPATTrees,RankandSelectDict,Succincter}.

\section{Evaluation}\label{sec:evaluation}
We now empirically evaluate \sketch.
\Cref{sec:evaluation_cms} examines the benefits of \sketch's techniques in detail through benchmarks of \sketchcms, while Sections~\ref{sec:evaluation_cs} and \ref{sec:evaluation_mg} demonstrate the framework's generality through benchmarks of \sketchcs and \sketchmg.
\Cref{sec:evaluation_join} further evaluates \sketch\ in the end-to-end application of join size estimation.
Across these sections, Experiments~\hyperlink{experiment:accuracy}{1}, \hyperlink{experiment:skew}{2}, \hyperlink{experiment:vale_tuning}{3}, \hyperlink{experiment:accuracy_unbiased}{6}, and \hyperlink{experiment:accuracy_mg}{8} focus on accommodating skew, while Experiments~\hyperlink{experiment:expansion}{4}, \hyperlink{experiment:contraction}{5}, \hyperlink{experiment:l2_size_function}{7}, and \hyperlink{experiment:untracked_key_insertions}{9} deal with \mbox{unbounded data growth}.

\textbf{Platform.}
We run the experiments on a Fedora~44 machine with an Intel Xeon w7-2495X processor (4.8~GHz) with~24 cores.
Our machine features an~80~KB L1 cache and a~2~MB L2 cache for each core, a~45~MB shared L3 cache, and~64~GB of RAM.

\textbf{Datasets and Workloads.}
Following prior work~\cite{CountMeanMin,SALSA,BitSense,PyramidSketch,StingySketch,AdaptiveCounterSplicing,CounterTree,TreeSensing,TailoredSketch,AugmentedSketch,HeavyGuardian,HeavyKeeper,WavingSketch,ElasticSketch,MVSketch,SpaceSaving,BatchUpdateMisraGries,BitMatcher,JigsawSketch,MicroscopeSketch,PSketch,TightSketch}, we conduct our evaluation in a single-threaded setting using three real-world~streams:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textsc{Kosarak}: 8M clicks from a news portal~\cite{Kosarak},
      anonymized as 8-byte integers. This dataset is highly skewed, resembling
      a \textsc{Zipfian} distribution with an exponent parameter of~$\approx
      3.5$.
    \item \textsc{WebDocs}: 300M words extracted from 1.7M online English
        documents~\cite{WebDocs}. Each word in this dataset is encoded as
        an 8-byte integer. This dataset follows a skewed distribution with a
        \textsc{Zipfian} exponent of~$\approx 1.0$.
    \item \textsc{CAIDA}: 27M anonymized 5-tuples describing the network
      connection of packets from 10 traces taken from backbone Internet links
      in 2018~\cite{CAIDA2018}. Each 5-tuple is represented as a 13-byte
      string. This dataset has a \textsc{Zipfian} exponent of~$\approx 2.0$.
\end{itemize}
We evaluate the accuracy of each baseline by querying it with each distinct key in the stream and compute the \emph{Average Absolute Error (AAE)} of the estimates from the ground-truth.
We focus on absolute errors rather than relative errors to keep our presentation consistent with the theoretical error bounds studied in the literature.
We have found the relative errors to behave similarly to the absolute errors.

\subsection{\sketchcms}\label{sec:evaluation_cms}

\textbf{Baselines.}
We compare \sketchcms to a traditional CMS, as well as the variants described in \Cref{sec:problem_analysis} that cater to skew.
We use SALSA~\cite{SALSA} to represent counter merging methods and Waving sketch~\cite{WavingSketch} to represent hybrid methods, as they are the most accurate and the fastest in their category.
For counter sharing methods, we employ the following three baselines: Stingy sketch~\cite{StingySketch} is the state of the art in terms of throughput since it implements the classic counter sharing design with a hierarchy of shared byte-aligned counters that prefetches the counters, as described in \Cref{sec:problem_analysis}.
Coding sketch~\cite{CodingSketch} is the most accurate counter sharing method that compresses its hierarchy of counters, trading performance for memory.
Tailored sketch~\cite{TailoredSketch} is the most space-efficient (and thus accurate) counter sharing method, as it probabilistically increments each counter in the classic hierarchy, thereby shortening the counters' length in exchange for introducing two-sided errors.
Among these six baselines, only Coding sketch is tunable, though its tuning is manual and complex.
This is because its compression algorithm obscures the level of skew in the workload to which the tuning should be adapted.
We implement \sketch\ in \texttt{C++} and use the open-source \texttt{C++} implementations of the baselines.
The aforementioned implementations are all single-threaded. We construct all FE sketches with~$d=3$ arrays.
This parameter setting is common in practice since it yields a confidence bound of~$\approx 95\%$, which is significantly higher than the confidence bounds resulting from~$d=1$ ($\approx 63\%$) and $d=2$ ($\approx 86\%$) while being comparable~to~$d=4$~($\approx 98\%$).

\hypertarget{experiment:accuracy}{\textbf{Experiment 1: Accuracy and Speed vs. Memory.}}
In \Cref{fig:accuracy_bench}, we feed each workload to each baseline while varying the allotted memory budget.
This experiment features bounded data growth, allowing us to focus on how well each baseline accommodates skew.
Each point on the curves represents a complete run of the experiment starting from scratch.
We tune Coding sketch and \sketchcms at each point to minimize their error and showcase their highest accuracy.
The table to the right of \Cref{fig:accuracy_bench} details the tuned parameters of \sketchcms---the number of counters per chunk~$c$ and the stub length~$s$.
We increase the memory budget by factors of 2 on the $x$-axis up to 4 MBs, though for the \textsc{Kosarak} dataset, we use smaller budgets due to the workload \mbox{trace being shorter}.

\Cref{fig:accuracy_bench}
shows that \sketchcms has a lower estimation error than most other baselines by
as much as an order of magnitude while providing similar or superior insertion
and query performance. This is because it extends overflowing counters without
affecting other counter values. Compared to the baseline with the closest
errors, i.e., SALSA, \sketchcms has lower errors by 40\% across the board while
providing faster insertions and queries by as much as~$3\times$ and
$2.4\times$. This performance improvement is due to \sketchcms's use of rank
and select primitives, as well as
Optimizations~\hyperlink{optimization:rank_and_select}{1} to
\hyperlink{optimization:horner_hack}{6} described in
\Cref{sec:accommodating_skew}. Coding
sketch exhibits slower insertion and query performance by~2 and 7 orders of
magnitude compared to the other baselines due to its decompression overheads.
As such, we henceforth exclude it from the experiments that evaluate
\sketchcms. When the memory budget is low under the \textsc{CAIDA} dataset,
Tailored sketch is more accurate than \sketchcms due to its probabilistic
incrementation strategy enabling it to store shorter counters in exchange for
introducing underestimations. Applying the same technique to \sketchcms yields
lower errors than Tailored sketch by~$2.38\times$, as shown by the dotted
curve.

\hypertarget{experiment:skew}{\textbf{Experiment 2: Accuracy under Varying Levels of Skew.}}
\Cref{fig:skew_vale_tuning_bench}{\nobreakdash-}A compares the estimation errors of the baselines under the same memory budget as the amount of skew in the data varies.
Here, we generate streams of 100M keys from a \textsc{Zipfian} distribution over 100K distinct keys.
We vary the exponent parameter, i.e., the skewness, on the~$x${\nobreakdash-}axis from~0.0 to 3.2, which correspond to a uniform and a highly skewed dataset, respectively.
Recall that, aside from Coding sketch (which we exclude from the experiment due to its poor performance), \sketchcms is the only tunable FE sketch. 
Thus, we allow it to adapt \counterencodingabbrv's tuning to the workload's skew.

\Cref{fig:skew_vale_tuning_bench}-A shows that \sketchcms provides the lowest error under all degrees of skew.
It outperforms all other baselines by an order of magnitude for more uniform workloads and by~$3\times$ for more skewed workloads.
This is because it exposes a tuning space that allows \counterencodingabbrv\ to save memory by employing longer and shorter stubs for more uniform and skewed workloads, respectively. 

\hypertarget{experiment:vale_tuning}{\textbf{Experiment 3: Memory Savings of Adaptively Tuning \counterencodingabbrv.}}
We demonstrate the need for adapting \counterencodingabbrv's tuning as the stream grows by comparing \sketchcms's memory footprint when using a fixed tuning to when it adapts the tuning.
We employ a total of 1M counters for both versions and use~$c=64$ counters per chunk and stubs of length~$s=6$ bits for the fixed tuning version.
\Cref{fig:skew_vale_tuning_bench}-B considers the \textsc{Zipfian} workloads from Experiment~\hyperlink{experiment:skew}{2} and varies the skew on the $x$-axis.
As shown, adaptively tuning \counterencodingabbrv\ bounds the number of allocated tails arrays and improves the memory footprint over using a fixed tuning by as much as~$5\times$.
With enough skew, the average extension length decreases, and the fixed tuning version of \sketchcms avoids allocating many tails arrays.
Nevertheless, it does not optimally leverage the shorter extension lengths and consumes~$\approx 10\%$ more memory than the adaptive version. 

\iftods
\begin{figure}[t]
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\begin{tabular}{cc}
    \hspace*{-6mm}
    \begin{minipage}{0.51\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{expansion_camera_ready.pdf}
        \vspace{-7mm}
        \captionof{figure}{\sketchcms achieves both a low error rate and a small memory

        \label{fig:expansion_bench}
    \end{minipage}
    &
    \begin{minipage}{0.47\textwidth}

        many keys~are~deleted.}
        \label{fig:contraction_bench}
    \end{minipage}
\end{tabular}

\fi

In this experiment, adaptations comprise less than~3\% of the processing time, as their overhead is amortized.
One can expect this overhead in mixed workloads with both insertions and deletions to be similar to our insertion-only workload.
This is because our workload triggers just as many adaptations as mixed workloads by growing the counters and causing many chunks to overflow.

\hypertarget{experiment:expansion}{\textbf{Experiment 4: Accuracy and Memory under Data Growth.}}
We now compare the accuracy and memory footprint of the baselines when the stream grows indefinitely.
Here, we allocate each baseline an initial memory footprint of 32KB and insert the \textsc{WebDocs} dataset.
\Cref{fig:expansion_bench} measures, for each baseline, its error and the ratio of its memory footprint to the stream's length.
We use the default configuration for \sketchcms, which has a sublinear power size function of the form~$W(N)=N^\alpha/\epsilon$ with $\alpha=0.5$ and $\epsilon=1$, as introduced in \Cref{sec:accommodating_unknown_stream_lengths}.
We also compare versions of \sketchcms with the exponent~$\alpha$ of the size function set to~$0.75$ and $1.0$.
\Cref{fig:expansion_bench} differentiates these versions by annotating each curve with the value of~$\alpha$ used. 

\Cref{fig:expansion_bench} shows that \sketchcms is the only FE sketch to exhibit sublinearly scaling estimation errors.
It achieves this by expanding its number of counters along with the stream.
This culminates in the three versions of \sketchcms (with exponents~$\alpha$ of $0.5$, $0.75$, and $1.0$) improving the error over the best baseline by one, two, and three orders of magnitude.
The right subfigure in \Cref{fig:expansion_bench} shows that, despite expanding the number of counters, \sketchcms maintains a modest memory footprint relative to the stream's length.
Furthermore, we have found that expansions take up only~0.5\% of the processing time, as they simply copy the counters sequentially.

\hypertarget{experiment:contraction}{\textbf{Experiment 5: Contractions.}}
\Cref{fig:contraction_bench} showcases deletions and contractions in \sketchcms.
Here, we insert the \textsc{CAIDA} dataset and subsequently delete keys in a random order while measuring the error and the memory footprint.
We only compare \sketchcms to CMS since all other baselines do not provide a deletion API.

As shown, \sketchcms maintains lower errors than a CMS of the same size by as much as two orders of magnitude.
This is because \counterencodingabbrv\ encodes the counter values in less space, allowing the storage of more counters.
As keys are deleted, \sketchcms contracts to save memory, while CMS wastes memory by remaining at the same size.
This enables achieving a lower memory footprint than CMS by~2--3 orders of magnitude while still being more accurate, despite the reduced number of counters slightly increasing the error.
Moreover, since contractions sequentially scan the FE sketch, similarly to expansions, they take up at most~0.5\% of the total execution time.

\subsection{\sketchcs}\label{sec:evaluation_cs}
We now turn to \sketchcs to demonstrate the applicability of \sketch\ beyond CMS.

\textbf{Baselines.}
We compare \sketchcs to a traditional CS and Waving sketch~\cite{WavingSketch}, which is representative of unbiased hybrid methods.
We also compare to Stingy sketch~\cite{StingySketch} and Coding sketch~\cite{CodingSketch} applied to CS, representing counter sharing methods with unbiased estimates.
We exclude SALSA~\cite{SALSA} as its library does not provide a variant with unbiased estimates.

\hypertarget{experiment:accuracy_unbiased}{\textbf{Experiment 6: Accuracy and Speed vs. Memory.}}
In this experiment, we employ the \textsc{CAIDA} dataset to evaluate the accuracy and speed of the baselines under various memory budgets.
We optimally tune Coding sketch and \sketchcs for this workload.
\Cref{fig:accuracy_unbiased_bench} shows that, by leveraging the memory savings of \counterencodingabbrv\ to store more counters, \sketchcs achieves lower errors than the next best baseline by as much as an order of magnitude.
It also has the fastest insertions while closely matching the query speed of the most performant sketch due to its use of Optimizations~\hyperlink{optimization:rank_and_select}{1} to
\hyperlink{optimization:horner_hack}{6} \mbox{from \Cref{sec:accommodating_skew}}.

\iftods
\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.50\columnwidth]{l2_size_function_camera_ready.pdf}
    \caption{By expanding based on the estimation variance, \sketchcs maintains
    a stable confidence bound.}
    \label{fig:l2_size_function_bench}
\end{figure}
\fi

\hypertarget{experiment:l2_size_function}{\textbf{Experiment 7: Expansion Based on Variance.}}
\Cref{fig:l2_size_function_bench} illustrates how expanding \sketchcs based on the growth of the estimation variance (as described in~\Cref{sec:cs}) instead of the number of keys enables a stable confidence bound.
We demonstrate this benefit by inserting~100M keys sampled from \textsc{Zipfian} distributions with increasing levels of skew.
We measure the P{\nobreakdash-}99 absolute estimation error rather than the average, since it captures the errors that exceed their confidence bounds, whereas averages obscure this information.
We also report the final memory footprint after ingesting the stream.

\ifnotods
\begin{figure*}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.86\textwidth]{join_size.pdf}
    \caption{\sketch\ provides the most accurate frequency estimates of table
    entries and the size of their join.}
    \label{fig:join_size_bench}
\end{figure*}
\fi


As shown in \Cref{fig:l2_size_function_bench}, expanding based on the estimation variance ensures stable tail errors in exchange for a higher memory footprint.
This is in contrast to expanding \sketchcs based on the number of keys, which yields tail errors that increase with skew.
The reason is that a more skewed dataset grows the variance more quickly, necessitating more frequent expansions to maintain a stable confidence bound.
As skew increases further, expanding based on the variance yields diminishing returns.
This is due to the stream's distinct keys dropping in number with skew, making keys less likely to map to the same counter and create severe errors.


\subsection{Join Size Estimation}\label{sec:evaluation_join}
We now demonstrate the benefits of \sketch\ in join size estimation.

\iftods
\begin{figure*}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{join_size_camera_ready.pdf}
    \caption{\sketch\ provides the most accurate frequency estimates of table
    entries and the size of their join.}
    \label{fig:join_size_bench}
\end{figure*}
\fi

\textbf{Dataset and Workload.}
We employ a 100 GB TPC-H workload~\cite{TPCH}.
We consider the \texttt{lineitem} and \texttt{orders} tables, incrementally insert rows into each, and periodically join them on their shared \texttt{ORDERKEY} column.
Following~\cite{JoinSizeEstimationFilterConditions}, each row in the \texttt{orders} table is duplicated~1--3 times to simulate a many-to-many join. 
We construct FE sketches tracking the count of each \texttt{ORDERKEY} value in these tables and predict their counts in the join by multiplying the per-table estimates.
We set the initial memory budget to~8MB ($\approx0.1\%$ of the data size) and distribute it to each sketch in proportion to the initial size of its table.

\textbf{Baselines.}
We compare CMS and CS with fixed-size and expandable versions of \sketchcms and \sketchcs.
The expandable \sketchcms and \sketchcs grow as a sublinear power (with a power of~0.75) of the tables.
We exclude MG and \sketchmg from this experiment because they return frequency estimates of zero for untracked keys, thereby causing severe underestimation of the join size when individual frequency estimates are multiplied.

\hypertarget{experiment:join_size}{\textbf{Experiment 10: Join Size Estimation.}}
\Cref{fig:join_size_bench} reports accuracy measurements for each table and the join result. 
As shown, the fixed-size versions of \sketchcms and \sketchcs attain higher accuracy than CMS and CS by multiple orders of magnitude due to their use of variable-length counters.
Moreover, the expandable \sketchcms and \sketchcs outperform their fixed-size counterparts by as much as an order of magnitude.
The fixed-size version of \sketchcs is more accurate than the fixed-size version of \sketchcms on each separate table.
This is because the workload in this experiment is almost uniform, leading to the positive and negative counts of colliding keys in \sketchcs to cancel out.
When allowed to expand, \sketchcms is more accurate than \sketchcs on individual tables, as collisions reduce with expansions, and \sketchcms does not store a sign bit for each counter.
Nevertheless, both versions of \sketchcs have more accurate join estimates than the corresponding version of \sketchcms.
This is due to \sketchcs returning unbiased estimates, which allows for multiplying them without compounding errors.


\subsection{\sketchmg}\label{sec:evaluation_mg}
We now turn our focus to evaluating \sketchmg under both skew and stream growth.

\ifnotods
\begin{figure*}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.7\textwidth]{accuracy_mg_arxiv.pdf}
    \hspace{1pt}
    \def\arraystretch{0.871}%  1 is the default, change whatever you need
    \small
    \begin{minipage}{0.28\textwidth}
    \setlength{\tabcolsep}{14.0pt}
    \begin{tabular}[b]{ccc} 
        \toprule 
        Memory [MB] & $c$ & $s$ \\ 
        \midrule 
        1/8 & 40 & 9 \\ 
        1/4 & 49 & 8 \\ 
        1/2 & 49 & 8 \\ 
        1 & 56 & 6 \\ 
        2 & 68 & 5 \\ 
        4 & 68 & 5 \\ 
        \bottomrule 
    \end{tabular} 
    \captionof{table}{Tuning \counterencodingabbrv's parameters for each plot point as in this table enables high performance and accuracy for \sketchmg.}
    \vspace{6.05cm}
    \end{minipage} \\
    \vspace*{-5.21cm}
    \caption{\sketchmg attains the highest accuracy while maintaining insertion and query performance comparable to the state of the art.
    The table above details how \sketchmg automatically tunes \counterencodingabbrv\ by listing the number of counters per chunk~$c$ and the stub length~$s$.}
    \label{fig:accuracy_mg_bench}
\end{figure*}
\fi

\textbf{Baselines.}
We consider two versions of \sketchmg: a scan-based version that scans the hash table to perform decrements and evictions, and a de-amortized version that handles these scans using global and local decrements and a compressed segment tree, as described in \Cref{sec:mg_sublime}.
Both versions use a Cuckoo hash table~\cite{CuckooHashing,CuckooHashingConference} with four slots per bucket.
We compare against a traditional scan-based MG implementation that uses the same Cuckoo hash table configuration.
We additionally include an MG variant that uses a min-heap to de-amortize the scans, as well as Space-Saving~\cite{SpaceSaving}, which de-amortizes MG's scans using a doubly linked list.
Finally, we include Waving sketch~\cite{WavingSketch}, a state-of-the-art hybrid sketch that employs a small, scan-based MG in each bucket of its hash table to track frequent keys.

\hypertarget{experiment:accuracy_mg}{\textbf{Experiment 8: Accuracy and Speed vs. Memory.}}
In this experiment, we consider a scenario with bounded data growth to isolate the benefits of using \counterencodingabbrv\ to compactly encode counters.
\Cref{fig:accuracy_mg_bench} reports the accuracy and performance of the baselines on the \textsc{CAIDA} dataset across various memory budgets.
Each point on the curves represents an independent run of the experiment starting from scratch.
We present measurements only for the \textsc{CAIDA} dataset, as we found that the \textsc{Kosarak} and \textsc{WebDocs} yield similar results.

\iftods
\begin{figure*}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.79\textwidth]{accuracy_mg.pdf}
    \hspace{1pt}
    \def\arraystretch{0.871}%  1 is the default, change whatever you need
    \small
    \begin{minipage}{0.19\textwidth}
    \setlength{\tabcolsep}{1.7pt}
    \begin{tabular}[b]{ccc} 
        \toprule 
        Memory [MB] & $c$ & $s$ \\ 
        \midrule 
        1/8 & 40 & 9 \\ 
        1/4 & 49 & 8 \\ 
        1/2 & 49 & 8 \\ 
        1 & 56 & 6 \\ 
        2 & 68 & 5 \\ 
        4 & 68 & 5 \\ 
        \bottomrule 
    \end{tabular} 
    \captionof{table}{Tuning \counterencodingabbrv's parameters for each plot point as in this table enables high performance and accuracy.}
    \vspace{6.05cm}
    \end{minipage} \\
    \vspace*{-5.71cm}
    \caption{\sketchmg attains the highest accuracy while maintaining insertion and query performance comparable to the state of the art.
    The table above details how \sketchmg automatically tunes \counterencodingabbrv\ by listing the number of counters per chunk~$c$ and the stub length~$s$.}
    \label{fig:accuracy_mg_bench}
\end{figure*}
\fi

As shown in the top-left subfigure of \Cref{fig:accuracy_mg_bench}, the scan-based version of \sketchmg achieves the lowest estimation errors across all memory budgets, with the de-amortized version following closely.
Both versions outperform MG, the most accurate baseline, by up to~2$\times$ and all other baselines by at least~4$\times$.
These improvements stem from \sketchmg's ability to track more keys using the space saved by compactly encoding counters with \counterencodingabbrv.
The de-amortized version achieves nearly the same accuracy and memory footprint as the scan-based version because its global and local decrements and compressed segment tree incur a total memory overhead of only~3 bits per counter, less than~5\% of the space required to store a key and its counter.

Since many applications use MG to identify the stream's heavy hitters~\cite{HeavyGuardian,HeavyKeeper,WavingSketch,NetworkAnomalyDetection1,NetworkAnomalyDetection2}, we also measure the average additive estimation error over the~$w$ most frequent keys, where~$w$ is the number of counters maintained by the baseline under evaluation.
We denote this metric by \emph{Top{\nobreakdash-}$w$ AAE}.
As shown in the top-middle subfigure of \Cref{fig:accuracy_mg_bench}, the gap in estimation error between \sketchmg and the baselines is approximately~1.5$\times$ larger under top{\nobreakdash-}$w$ AAE than under conventional AAE.
This is because all baselines return an estimate of zero for untracked keys, making their conventional AAE measurements more similar despite differences in the number of keys they track.

Because all baselines employ Cuckoo hashing, they achieve similar query performance, as shown in the top-right subfigure of \Cref{fig:accuracy_mg_bench}.
For insertions, the scan-based version of \sketchmg, MG, and Waving sketch achieve the lowest average latencies, as shown in the bottom-left subfigure.
This is because these sketches avoid maintaining the auxiliary data structures required by de-amortized implementations.
However, the scans performed by MG and the scan-based version of \sketchmg lead to maximum insertion latencies that grow linearly with the sketch size, as shown in the bottom-right subfigure.
The de-amortized version of \sketchmg addresses this limitation, reducing maximum insertion latency by up to two orders of magnitude to match Waving sketch and the fastest de-amortized baseline.
Moreover, as the memory budget increases, its average insertion latency is only~50\% higher than those of the scan-based baselines, and is~35\% and~10\% lower than those of the de-amortized MG implementation based on min-heaps and doubly linked lists, respectively.
These results demonstrate that \sketchmg's compressed segment tree preserves amortized constant insertion time while reducing the worst-case insertion complexity from linear to logarithmic in the sketch size.

\iftods
\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.55\columnwidth]{expansion_mg.pdf}
    \caption{By expanding based on the number of untracked-key insertions rather than the stream length, \sketchmg controls both the estimation error and the memory footprint as the stream grows.}
    \label{fig:expansion_mg_bench}
\end{figure}
\fi

\hypertarget{experiment:untracked_key_insertions}{\textbf{Experiment 9: Accuracy and Memory under Data Growth.}}
We now evaluate how \sketchmg accommodates stream growth.
We focus on the de-amortized version of \sketchmg, as its accuracy is similar to that of the scan-based version.
We benchmark its default configuration, which uses a sublinear power size function of the form~$W(M)=M^\alpha/\epsilon$ with~$\alpha=0.5$ and~$\epsilon=1$, where~$M$ denotes the number of untracked-key insertions.
We also include variants \mbox{with~$\alpha=0.75$} and~$1.0$.
To demonstrate the benefits of expanding based on the number of untracked-key insertions~$M$ rather than the stream length~$N$, we compare against corresponding variants of \sketchmg that instead use~$W(N)=N^\alpha/\epsilon$ as their size function.
We include MG as a baseline but omit the other baselines from the previous experiment, as MG achieved the highest accuracy among them.
As in Experiment~\hyperlink{experiment:expansion}{4} of \Cref{sec:evaluation_cms}, we initialize each sketch with a memory footprint of~32KB and feed it the \textsc{WebDocs} dataset.
\Cref{fig:accuracy_mg_bench} reports the estimation error and memory footprint of each sketch as the stream grows.
The number next to each \sketchmg line indicates the value of~$\alpha$ used in its size function.

\ifnotods
\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{expansion_mg.pdf}
    \caption{By expanding based on the number of untracked-key insertions rather than the stream length, \sketchmg controls both the estimation error and the memory footprint as the stream grows.}
    \label{fig:expansion_mg_bench}
\end{figure}
\fi


As shown in the left subfigure of \Cref{fig:expansion_mg_bench}, all \sketchmg variants achieve lower estimation errors than MG by as much as several orders of magnitude, since they expand as the stream grows.
Moreover, they achieve this while maintaining a small memory footprint, as shown in the right subfigure.
The figure also shows that increasing the exponent~$\alpha$ of the size function reduces the estimation error in exchange for a larger memory footprint, since it causes the sketch to expand more rapidly.

Expanding based on the total number of insertion~$N$ rather than the number of untracked-key insertions~$M$ also improves accuracy, but may incur a larger memory footprint than necessary to achieve a target estimation error.
The reason is that the former policy disregards how insertions of already-tracked keys do not increase the estimation error.
This trade-off is particularly evident for~$\alpha=1.0$ in \Cref{fig:expansion_mg_bench}.
When expanding based on~$N$ with~$\alpha=1.0$, \sketchmg's estimation error decreases toward zero as the stream grows.
In contrast, expanding based on~$M$ with~$\alpha=1.0$ maintains a stable estimation error as intended while reducing the memory footprint by an order of magnitude compared to expanding based on~$N$.

\section{Conclusion}
We introduced \sketch, the first framework for generalizing a frequency estimation sketch to adapt to the skew and length of the stream.
We showed that \sketch\ improves the accuracy and memory of FE sketches beyond the state of the art while maintaining high performance.
Although \sketch\ is currently single-threaded, we expect significant performance gains from parallelization (e.g., through employing thread-local FE sketches to ingest subsets of the stream and periodically merging them into a unified FE sketch for answering queries~\cite{FastConcurrentDataSketches,FPGASketching}).
Studying the interplay of concurrent updates with expansions, contractions, and adaptive tuning would be an interesting direction of future work.
More broadly, \sketch\ opens a new research avenue on expandable data sketches applicable to other core statistics, such as quantiles and cardinalities, which exhibit similar error growth under workloads with growing datasets.
This marks a fundamental shift from fixed-size sketches with degrading accuracy to adaptive structures that maintain stable accuracy for always-on analytics.
